PATTERN 6 - the CLI's own lifecycle verbs get bypassed. S03 ran 413 Bash calls, 13 `docker run -d` and 21 defensive `docker rm -f`, and never once called `cuttle up`/`down`/`open`. When an agent is testing an image it reaches for docker directly and then reimplements port-conflict and readiness handling badly. `cuttle up --image` needs to be the documented path AND needs to block until CDP is genuinely ready.

PATTERN 7 - the browser's most reliable use turned out to be as an authenticated fetch origin, not a clicking surface. Both S24 and S06 abandoned a stuck UI widget and got the exact answer instantly via in-page `fetch()` (`200 :: {...}`, `409 {\"error\":\"This repository has already been submitted...\"}`, `429 rate_limit_exceeded`); S05 twice bypassed a dead DOM by hitting the app's own URL directly. Three agents rediscovered this independently. Worth promoting to documented practice rather than leaving as a last resort.

NEGATIVE FINDINGS worth recording: across all four source sessions there were ZERO cookie-banner fights, ZERO captcha/Cloudflare walls, ZERO shadow-DOM blindness in Angular Material, ZERO JS dialogs, and ZERO driver-spawns-its-own-browser attach failures. The persistent profile did its job - the agents were already logged into a cloud provider, a note-taking app and an internal tool. The real blockers were focus, shell quoting, cold start and unverified mutations, i.e. ergonomics, not stealth.

**H16.** These two sessions ran CONCURRENTLY against the same cuttle browser on 2026-08-07, and neither agent ever realised the other was an agent. Session S05 saw a SaaS console tabs appear and concluded "something else is driving that browser" then "we're both driving the same browser"; session S06 saw a note-taking app/a cloud provider/an internal tool tabs appear and concluded "another session has opened tabs in this browser and stolen document focus". Each blamed the human. Both lost work: S05 abandoned a fully filled a note-taking app OAuth form and handed the task back to the user for two hours; S06 had its CodeMirror config corrupted into invalid JSON. cuttle ships `--context` and `context ls|add` for exactly this, and NEITHER session used them - because `cuttle up`'s briefing never mentions contexts, and its only concurrency guidance is "NEVER launch your own browser", which reads as a warning against a second browser, not a warning about a second driver. That single omission is the most expensive documentation defect visible across the batch.

Second cross-session pattern, equally sharp: screenshot usage is pure luck. Same model, same machine, same day, same driver, same user. Session S06 took 2 screenshots in 117 browser calls and both times it immediately resolved an ambiguity. Session S05 took 0 in 183 calls and instead authored 132 bespoke JavaScript DOM-scraping programs, 27 getBoundingClientRect coordinate computations, and 21 raw mousemove/mousedown/mouseup gestures - it rebuilt a vision-and-click system in JS because nothing in cuttle's briefing suggests looking at the page is an option. cuttle owns a KasmVNC framebuffer and has no screenshot verb; the briefing's only visual affordance is "hand the user the viewer link", which frames seeing the screen as a human activity rather than an agent one.

Third: both sessions independently discovered that input does not go where the driver thinks it goes, and neither could get a truthful answer about it. S05 found `visibilityState: hidden` and correctly inferred the foreground rule after three separate episodes. S06 found `document.hasFocus(): true` and was actively misled - it published a root cause it could not have verified. Those two signals disagree specifically because cuttle pins `Emulation.setFocusEmulationEnabled` per page. A cuttle feature intended to keep background tabs alive has made the standard agent focus diagnostic unreliable, and there is no cuttle-provided replacement.

Fourth, quieter: neither session ran `cuttle skill`. Both went straight to the driver's own SKILL.md. The briefing lists the driver docs command inline with the attach command and relegates the cuttle guide to its last line. Every cuttle-specific gotcha in this batch - foreground input routing, shared-browser collisions, snapshot leaking secret values via aria-label, the about:blank keep-alive tab occupying an ordinal - was rediscovered empirically at a cost of 60+ turns, and all four are things a guide could have stated in four lines that nobody read.

**H17.** Three patterns only visible across both sessions:

(1) FRICTION IN THE CLICK PATH SILENTLY DISABLES CUTTLE'S CORE VALUE. In both sessions the driver's high-level click/fill no-opped on non-native widgets (Angular in the tax portal, React on a professional social network), and in both the escape hatch was raw in-page JS: 65 acting `eval` calls in session 1, 22 plus 28 synthetic-MouseEvent dispatches in session 2. In-page `el.click()` / `dispatchEvent` / native-value-setter never touches CDP Input, so cuttle's humanizer, settle gate, typo injection and post-click aria verify are all bypassed - exactly on the hardest pages, and with no signal that it is happening. Whatever else is fixed, the click path needs a humanized force-click so agents never have to reach for `eval` to act.

(2) READING STATE COSTS MORE THAN CHANGING IT. Session 1: 261 snapshots + 145 read-only evals = 46% of 892 calls. Session 2: 199 read-only evals + 29 snapshots. Neither session trusted the a11y snapshot for form state; session 1 escalated all the way to a file round-trip (save draft -> `cuttle downloads` -> grep XML) as its only believable ground truth, ~14 times. A `cuttle state`/`read` verb returning form values keyed by stable ids would collapse the single largest cost in both transcripts. The related tell: screenshots were used 9 times in 892 calls in session 1 and only after the user demanded it, because the output path had to be discovered by grepping driver stdout.

(3) EVERY BOUNDARY CROSSING IS UNDOCUMENTED AND WAS DISCOVERED THE EXPENSIVE WAY. Host filesystem -> container (both sessions independently ran `docker cp` into the cuttle container, and in both cases the host-side path would have worked); container -> host network (`host.docker.internal` failed, dev server had to be rebound to a LAN IP); driver -> driver (a beforeunload dialog owned by one driver session wedged the other, and the second driver's error blamed the tab); identity -> identity (the agent launched a non-cuttle browser for a second login until the user pointed it at `cuttle up --name`); and browser session -> site session (cookies persist, server sessions expire, and nothing surfaces the difference - it cost a lost multi-step wizard and nearly cost a lost tax declaration). Every one of these is a cuttle-layer concern that no driver can fix, and each was learned by burning turns rather than by reading SKILL.md.

Also worth noting for the human-handoff design: across both sessions the human took the keyboard/mouse in the viewer at least four times (typed a a national tax id, dragged a slider, entered credentials twice) and every single time the agent was blind to it until told in chat. cuttle sits between the viewer and the browser and is the only component that could report 'the human just acted'.

**H18.** Three patterns visible only across the pair:

1. **The stealth invariant one session fought for, the other destroyed with one convenience call.** Session S03 spent an entire release cycle establishing per-seed screen/window coherence (dropping the 2560x1440 pair, pinning taskbar height, adding dropMaximizeIfSized, regenerating the golden) because "outerWidth 1536 around innerWidth 780 isn't a windowless launch, it's a visibly impossible window." One week later, session S04 ran `playwright-cli resize 1440 900` to make a screenshot look nicer, never restored it, and did all its logged-in work in that state. Nothing rejected it, warned, re-applied the seed size, or logged it. cuttle treats window geometry as identity on the way in and as a free-for-all on the way out.

2. **Users are maintaining a private cuttle cookbook because the product has no memory, and the cookbook is leaky by construction.** `a project directory/pjs/career/CLAUDE.md` now carries two hand-written "platform ops (cuttle browser)" sections (a freelance marketplace, Aug 5-6; a freelance marketplace, Aug 14). Most of their content is *generic cuttle knowledge*, not site knowledge - "ASSUME LOGGED OUT", "Drive the API, not the DOM", "mint the TOTP and submit in ONE chained command", "modals cache their data, RELOAD after an API write", "file input index 0 vs 1, then Crop". And three lines in the a freelance marketplace section are near-miss credential-leak rules the user had to derive alone: "`run-code` ECHOES ITS SOURCE to stdout, so any secret-bearing run-code would leak", "Do NOT snapshot the page between fill and submit (nothing auto-redacts the field value in snapshots)", "playwright-cli 0.1.17 removed `process` from the run-code sandbox, so the old process.env fill is dead". SKILL.md covers the happy path of secrets; every sharp edge around it was found the hard way, per user, per site.

3. **Both sessions failed the same way: they trusted a check that silently returns the wrong answer, and only caught it by luck.** S03 trusted `document.fonts.check()` (unpatched, returns true for everything) and published a wrong font finding; it also trusted a mid-run pod sample and published "panic confirmed fixed" two minutes before the daemon panicked. S04 trusted a `PATCH .../skills` that returned 200 and changed nothing, and trusted `x.thumbnail`/`x.images` field names that do not exist. In every case the recovery was the same discipline - "read state back after you change it" - which is already in SKILL.md and is the single highest-value line in it. The gap is that cuttle offers no primitive that makes read-back cheap: no `cuttle verify`, no crash counter, no per-seed status. The agents are doing verification by hand-built probe, and getting it wrong at roughly the rate you would expect.

4. **Minor but consistent: neither session ever encountered a JS dialog, and neither ever opened the VNC viewer voluntarily.** The one human handoff that happened (session 2's resume swap) was initiated by the human rejecting a tool call, not by the agent asking. The viewer is cuttle's headline human-in-the-loop feature and it appeared zero times in 250 browser turns.
## Appendix I - Per-session summaries

What each of the 45 cuttle-driving sessions was actually attempting, whether it succeeded,
and how much of its browser effort went to friction rather than the task. These were
rewritten by a dedicated de-identification pass: every technical detail is verbatim, while
service names, product names, people and identifiers are replaced with category
descriptions. A denylist scrub was not sufficient here - the identifying material was
open-ended proper nouns, including a third party's name.

**S21.** Agent attached playwright-cli to a warm cuttle session (cuttle 0.11.4, Chrome 148) and audited a SaaS console's AI marketing dashboard: expanding six agent cards, cycling four analytics tabs (SEO/Links/Technical/GEO), and opening a locked 'citation gap' modal. It succeeded and produced a full verdict for the user. About 22 browser calls over 3.5 minutes, with no human correction.

*Wasted effort:* ~25% - one blocked click on an invisible modal backdrop plus three stale-ref errors that forced re-find/re-snapshot round trips.

**S22.** After diagnosing a cloud project suspension over the provider's CLI, the agent used cuttle (0.11.4) + playwright-cli to open the cloud provider console's appeal form in a new tab of the user's warm session, fill a 1049-char appeal, get user approval, submit it, then later open the billing report to read cost figures. Both browser tasks succeeded, but almost every step needed a workaround: run-code instead of typing, raw eval instead of screenshots, and a webmail client as the source of truth for whether the submit actually landed.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% - roughly 10 of ~30 browser-related calls went to failed heredoc plumbing, a silently-failed screenshot, a screenshot timeout, and re-verifying a submission whose confirmation dialog vanished before it could be read.

**S05.** The agent drove cuttle (0.11.0, CDP :9222) with playwright-cli to configure a cloud provider's OAuth setup (add redirect URIs, publish the consent screen to Production), complete two OAuth consent flows into a self-hosted internal-tool gateway, create a public OAuth integration on a note-taking app, and capture the resulting client secret into a password manager - all against the user's live logged-in accounts. It eventually succeeded on every browser objective, but only after abandoning playwright-cli's own click/fill verbs for hand-rolled `--raw eval` + getBoundingClientRect + raw mousemove/mousedown/mouseup coordinate clicking, and after one whole phase (the note-taking app's connection form) was destroyed mid-work by another session driving the same browser. 184 playwright-cli invocations total, zero screenshots.

*Wasted effort:* Roughly half of the ~184 browser calls. The agent spent them re-locating elements after ref invalidation, discovering that its tab was backgrounded, re-clicking buttons that had silently done nothing, and rebuilding forms that were clobbered by a concurrent session.

**S06.** The agent drove cuttle (0.11.0) with playwright-cli to get an internal project listed on a SaaS console: sign in via code-host OAuth (handed off to the human through the VNC viewer for credentials), submit the server, configure the SaaS console's structured Dockerfile builder, run and poll remote builds, and cut two releases; it also submitted to a second directory site. Everything succeeded. The dominant friction was that the SaaS console's UI is animated (so Playwright actionability checks never pass) and its config fields are CodeMirror editors that could not be edited reliably, plus a concurrent session that stole document focus and silently turned 'replace' into 'append', producing invalid JSON in a live config.

*Wasted effort:* Roughly 40% of the ~110 browser calls. The CodeMirror editing problem alone consumed ~12 calls and 8 generated scratch scripts on first encounter and 5 more retries when it recurred hours later, and remote-build polling burned another ~25 calls.

**S03.** An overnight cuttle upgrade-validation session (an internal services repo on 0.1.0 -> cuttle 0.11.x). The agent drove cuttle browsers over CDP through a Playwright-based stealth harness and hand-written tsx/raw-CDP probe scripts (48 CDP-targeting shell commands, 281 cuttle-related commands, no driver CLI), across local qemu, a native amd64 host over ssh, a native arm64 local run, and a deployed canary. It succeeded: the CDP contract break was fixed upstream, three cuttle releases were cut (0.11.1/0.11.2/0.11.3, two of them purely to fix bugs found or introduced along the way), and prod was deployed and verified 33/33. But most of the night went to friction: a farm-killing daemon panic the agent could neither see nor reproduce, a silently-corrupting state capture, and an environment where most targets were untestable locally.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns. Two of the three releases (0.11.2, 0.11.3) existed only to chase a panic the agent could not observe or reproduce; three separate repro rigs were built and abandoned; the whole first phase was spent reverse-engineering a blocked CDP method that had no opt-out.

**S04.** The agent logged into the user's real account on a freelance marketplace through cuttle (password-manager creds + TOTP, playwright-cli 0.1.17 over CDP at 127.0.0.1:9222) and rebuilt the whole profile: headline, bio, experience level, work history dates, two new jobs, 27 skills, 8 project cards with generated logo images, links and resume. It then swept the whole job board (120 jobs) via the site's private API and produced a TSV baseline. The task succeeded, but ~295 tool calls were spent, the overwhelming majority of them inline `--raw eval` JavaScript rather than driver verbs, and the human had to correct the agent three times on things it could not see.

*Wasted effort:* ~35-40% of browser turns. Screenshot verification failed repeatedly and was abandoned; file upload took ~15 calls to find a working path; a whole skills-save attempt silently no-op'd; company creation cost ~10 failed API probes before falling back to the UI.

**S07.** The agent drove cuttle (via playwright-cli over CDP on 127.0.0.1:9222) to complete a wholesale retailer's 4-step customer-card registration wizard - a CRM platform front end built on a CRM component framework - at a third-party site, then stored a record in a password manager and drafted a follow-up email. It eventually succeeded (application submitted and confirmed by email), but only after the cuttle container crashed mid-form, ~20 tool calls fighting a silently-failing Continue button, and a mid-session meta-argument with the human about whether the agent was using its browser tooling competently. It also missed the form's file-upload field entirely, which the human caught after submission.

**S08.** Mostly an infra session (a self-hosted audiobook library server plus its companion request and indexer services), but three account registrations were done through cuttle: an ebook library site, a book-tracking site, and a media-index tracker site. The ebook library succeeded quickly; the book-tracking site stalled because password fills silently typed literal variable names instead of secrets, causing the agent to misdiagnose a 'cuttle secret-substitution truncation bug' and hand the task back to the human via the VNC viewer; the human refused the handoff and the agent then fixed it by re-attaching. The media-index tracker required reading two image captchas from screenshots and defeating a reCAPTCHA widget. All three accounts were eventually created by the agent unaided.

**S17.** A ~5-hour launch-day session on a large link-aggregator site. The agent attached playwright-cli to cuttle (http://127.0.0.1:9222) against the user's logged-in account on that site and used it purely as a read tool: polling post state, comment trees, and community rules by running in-page fetch() of the site's .json endpoints from the logged-in tab. It diagnosed a removal by an automated moderation bot, monitored comments on a 5-minute loop, and fed the human reply material. It succeeded at every read task, but the human performed 100% of the writes (post, delete, upvote, 6 replies) and supplied 12 screenshots because the agent could not see the page.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of browser turns. Almost all read attempts worked, but tab-ownership errors, the tab-verb hunt, the monitor dying, and the fallback-to-an-internal-research-tool detour were pure overhead on top of a workflow that was already a hand-rolled substitute for missing verbs.

**S18.** A long Windows-support engineering session (734 Bash calls) where cuttle was used for exactly one task the CLI could not do: minting a classic personal access token through a code host's web UI and piping it into the code-host CLI's `secret set <redacted-id>` command. The user explicitly authorized it ('q2 drive it'). The agent got as far as the code host's sudo-mode wall, stalled there for roughly 36 hours until the human tapped the mobile prompt, then completed the token flow and closed the tab. The browser portion succeeded but was the single longest-blocked item in the session.

*Wasted effort:* ~40% of the browser turns (roughly 12 of 29 playwright-cli calls) went to the stale-state-file failure, the duplicate tab, the DOM hunt for the expiry control and submit button, and title-polling - none of it the actual task.

**S20.** A payments/analytics audit. cuttle was used for two things the APIs could not do: scraping the query ids off a blockchain-analytics dashboard page (that site has no endpoint that lists a dashboard's queries) and reading billing in a crypto exchange's developer portal after a 2FA handoff to the human. Both eventually succeeded; the developer-portal navigation was the messy part. The rest of the session (rebuilding a metrics dashboard onto Postgres) had no browser involvement.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of the ~22 browser turns went to nav discovery, failed clicks, blind sleeps, and restoring the tab - the two actual data reads were maybe 5 calls.

**S01.** An 8-hour, 391-message session driving a national tax portal (an annual income-tax declaration SPA, the periodic VAT declaration, self-employed receipt issuance, and a government social-security portal) through cuttle with playwright-cli on session `default`, plus a second cuttle instance (`--name <redacted-id>`, ports 9223/6081) for a family member's tax account. 779 Bash calls, 628 of them playwright-cli: 245 snapshot, 208 eval, 69 click, only 6 screenshot. It succeeded - the income-tax declaration was submitted (confirmation <redacted-id>), two VAT declarations filed, three receipts issued, one social-security quarterly filed - but the agent was effectively blind for most of it, driving custom Angular widgets by raw JS and verifying every single change by downloading the form's XML and grepping it. The human repeatedly had to take the keyboard.

*Wasted effort:* ~45-55% of browser turns. The agent could not see the page (6 screenshots against 453 snapshot/eval probes), so it re-derived state by DOM probing and the save control-XML round trips after nearly every field, and lost long stretches to invisible native dialogs, silently-no-op widget clicks, and secrets that were typed as literal placeholder names.

**S02.** A three-day, 433-message career-lane session (Aug 6-8) that used cuttle heavily for a professional social network (company page creation, live profile reads, a full projects/skills/experience cleanup via the real UI), a freelance marketplace platform (board sweeps and profile date edits via its API and UI), and self-review screenshots of a third-party dev site. 335 playwright-cli / agent-browser calls, of which 223 were eval/run-code against only 4 clicks - the agent almost entirely abandoned the drivers' high-level verbs and drove raw JS. Everything asked for was eventually accomplished, but two long episodes were pure friction, and one of the agent's own experiments logged the user's professional-social-network session out of cuttle.

*Wasted effort:* ~40% of browser turns. Two episodes dominate: 58 calls over 19 minutes chasing a phantom 'cuttle cannot render the professional social network' limitation that was really a background tab, and a cookie-replay experiment that killed the logged-in session and had to be recovered.

**S09.** Career/incident-response session that turned into a malware-abuse-reporting run. The agent used cuttle (playwright-cli attach --cdp=http://127.0.0.1:9222) to drive four live abuse forms (a hosting provider, a code host, a package registry, a professional social network), read a profile menu on the professional social network, and capture evidence screenshots of a DM thread there. The package-registry and code-host reports were filed successfully; the hosting provider's form could not be submitted at all and was abandoned for email; the DM thread could only be captured as text, not pixels. 64 playwright-cli invocations total.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of browser turns. Roughly half the calls were raw `--raw eval` DOM surgery working around driver verbs that did not work on React forms, plus a ~12-call dead end on a form whose Submit silently did nothing.

**S10.** A fake-interview malware incident: the agent statically analysed a malicious repo, then used cuttle to file abuse reports (a code host, a hosting provider, and a dedicated support form on a professional social network), capture code-view screenshots on the code host as evidence, and pull a profile from the professional social network via a background-tab call to that site's internal API. All reports were eventually filed, but the hosting provider's form and the support form each cost a long detour, and the agent had to guide the human click-by-click through the social network's in-app report dialogs because it could not see the human's own browser. 59 browser invocations.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns. Two forms (the hosting provider's attachment step, the support form's multi-file upload) consumed most of the session's browser time, and the agent's own retrospective lists the browser mechanics as 'the browser mechanics that cost the most time today'.

**S11.** Career research session that used cuttle for three browser jobs: filing an abuse report to a note-taking app about a malicious page, an API sweep of a freelance marketplace via in-page fetch, and a full email-OTP login to a talent-network site followed by editing and saving the user's live profile (bio, roles, skills, proof-of-work link, two references). All three succeeded, but the OTP login had to be improvised and the profile edits produced repeated false-failure timeouts. 57 browser invocations.

*Wasted effort:* ~25% of browser turns. The OTP handoff and the false-timeout verification loop are the two clusters; everything else was ordinary form driving.

**S12.** A long infrastructure session in an internal services repo whose central subject was cuttle itself: repinning the local cuttle image to arm64, deleting the CUTTLE_SIDECAR_HOST rewrite, renaming the production browser Service to cuttle, and driving cuttle repeatedly through the stealth harness and a cluster canary. It also chased a multi-hour mystery - 'Target page, context or browser has been closed' - through persona, memory, seed-collision and mesh-VPN theories before finding a mirrord agent panic. The release itself took the production browser farm down for ~4 minutes on a cuttle-specific env-var collision. Two issues came out of it, including cuttle issue #45.

*Wasted effort:* ~50% of the cuttle-related turns. The `Target page... has been closed` hunt alone consumed hours and three subagents; the production outage and the two-way mode-flip defect were each found only by running the thing.

**S44.** Backend work on an internal fetch/scrape backend service: root-causing why the internal web-fetch tool could not fetch a public cloud-hosted document, benchmarking against a commercial scraping API, discovering and shipping a live SSRF fix, and writing an 8-phase improvement plan. cuttle was used as infrastructure, not as a page-driving tool: the agent brought the local cuttle container up over docker compose, curled its CDP `/json/version`, inspected its image arch and compose pinning, and ran the fetch cascade whose stealth tier dials cuttle over CDP. It succeeded at the backend goals; every cuttle interaction was operational friction rather than page automation.

*Wasted effort:* Roughly 30-40% of the cuttle-touching turns were friction rather than the task: hand-rolled readiness, the compose-vs-Tilt detour the human had to correct, skipping the purpose-built smoke check, and the doomed 51s render attempts on an unrenderable target. The backend work itself was efficient; every loss was in operating and observing the browser farm.

**S13.** Career/finance advisory session that, at 19:34 on 2026-08-14, turned into browser work when the user said "do that now yourself through cuttle". The agent ran `cuttle up`, attached playwright-cli to 127.0.0.1:9222, and did three tasks on a freelance marketplace platform: change profile availability 40h -> 10h, verify the payouts-provider payment method on one mission, and read + capture the platform's builder terms of service. All three succeeded, but the availability edit alone took ~14 exploratory driver calls, and the final Save had to be fired by injected JavaScript because the button never appeared in the accessibility snapshot.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of the 54 browser calls. The bulk went to hunting for an edit affordance the agent could not see and to slicing 1600-line accessibility snapshots with sed/rg.

**S14.** The user asked the agent to scrape transactions from a consumer banking app through cuttle (succeeded in one call via an existing script over CDP), then explicitly asked it to log into a consumer banking web app using password-manager credentials "without exposing my creds" and pull the current month's movements. The login worked and 18 rows were imported, but the secrets path leaked three of seven authentication-code digits into the transcript, the agent had to tell the user to rotate the code, and the humanized click on the bank's search button timed out and silently never fired.

*Wasted effort:* ~50% of ~60 browser calls. Secrets/redaction rework, the wrong-boxes OTP entry, the timed-out search click plus a 15-iteration poll, the masked-date refill, and one full redo of the search after navigation each cost multiple calls.

**S15.** Mostly a stress-test of an internal research tool, but at 12:19 the user said "update the directory listing to show 14 tools" and the agent drove cuttle for ~12 minutes: `cuttle up`, attach playwright-cli, log into a third-party site via code-host OAuth, edit the server description and repo field, re-publish the MCP endpoint to trigger a rescan, and verify all 14 tools appear. It succeeded, but lost calls to a deliberately-delayed OAuth Authorize button, a lazily-rendered tool list, and an invisible save confirmation.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of 40 browser calls, concentrated in three can't-see-the-page episodes.

**S16.** A meta-session: the user asked the agent to mine two weeks of agent-session history for how agents actually interacted with cuttle, stress-test a prior postmortem's six rules, and decide whether defaulting to run-code helps. Three Opus subagents read ~2,100 driver calls across 11 cuttle sessions. The agent then root-caused the long-standing 'playwright-cli click always times out' bug (hidden tab -> no rAF -> Playwright's stable check never settles), shipped four daemon fixes plus a SKILL.md rewrite as v0.11.4, and validated on arm64 locally and on a real amd64 host. It drove cuttle heavily throughout to measure before/after.

*Wasted effort:* ~30% of this session's own browser calls (failed traps, shell-quoting workarounds, a blocked context creation). But the session's real value is that it quantifies the wasted effort of the whole preceding corpus: the friction agent found roughly a third of ~2,100 driver calls went to friction rather than task.

**S23.** Agent drove cuttle (playwright-cli over CDP at 127.0.0.1:9222) to log into a freelance marketplace platform and sweep 38 open missions for the user's job search. It succeeded, but only after re-authenticating by hand-injecting the password via raw JS and fighting a multi-attempt Sign In click; nearly all data extraction was done by calling the marketplace's API with fetch() from inside eval rather than reading the page.

*Wasted effort:* ~60% of browser turns. Roughly 10 of 17 browser calls went to login mechanics (password injection, ambiguous Sign In button, sleep-poll for navigation) and CLI verb discovery, not to the mission sweep itself.

**S25.** Long career-pipeline session: read two job descriptions hosted on a note-taking app through cuttle after the internal fetch tool failed, then re-attached later to sweep a freelance marketplace's board and pull mission detail for two applications. Both goals were achieved, but the container had died between uses, attach errored on first try, and the actual marketplace application form was filled by the human by hand - the agent only produced text to paste.

*Wasted effort:* ~40% of browser turns: container restart, a failed attach, three jq parse failures on eval output, and hand-rolled setTimeout waits for the note-taking app's SPA.

**S26.** Short session evaluating one mission on a freelance marketplace. The agent attached to cuttle, found itself logged out, re-authenticated, and pulled the mission via the site API. It succeeded, but during login it ran a page snapshot that printed the user's marketplace password in cleartext into the transcript, and it then had to retry the Sign In click blind because it could not read the on-page error.

*Wasted effort:* ~50% of browser turns went to login (fill, JS password injection, two Sign In attempts, two 8-second polls, two snapshots) before any mission data was read.

**S27.** A freelance marketplace board check for new missions. The agent attached to cuttle, found the session expired (401 on the refresh-token call), re-logged in with the same JS password hack, then pulled the whole board and full mission detail for one mission via the site's API from page context. The sweep succeeded; the friction was all in attach, login, and verb discovery.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns: a wrong-page attach, a wrong verb, the login recipe, and a --help read before the actual board pull.

**S28.** Event-intelligence session: the user twice asked explicitly for cuttle ("check in cuttle pls all people attending", "can you analyze his connections on the professional social network? use cuttle"). The agent pulled a 27-person guest list from behind a login on an event-hosting site and read a profile on a professional social network. It got the answers, but only by clicking a modal open and then doing shell archaeology on the snapshot YAML files on disk, because the snapshot output was too big to read inline.

*Wasted effort:* ~55% of browser turns: 7 of 12 browser-related calls were shell forensics over .playwright-cli/page-*.yml rather than page interaction, plus one dead-end API call.

**S16.** The one session in this batch that actually drove the live cuttle browser (container 0.11.3 on 127.0.0.1:9222). Task: empirically root-cause why `playwright-cli click` hangs on 'waiting for element to be visible, enabled and stable'. It succeeded completely - proved the cause is zero requestAnimationFrame in non-active tabs combined with playwright's noDefaults:true CDP-attach path skipping Emulation.setFocusEmulationEnabled, produced a reversible causality matrix, and cleaned up all 10 tabs it created.

*Wasted effort:* ~30% of its browser turns went to building instrumentation cuttle does not provide (tab foreground truth, per-tab frame counts) and to undoing measurement contamination caused by the driver itself.

**S29.** Agent checked a freelance marketplace's mission board for new postings through cuttle (playwright-cli attached to 127.0.0.1:9222), driving the site's private JSON API from inside the page with `--raw eval` rather than touching the DOM. It succeeded, but the human then complained that every such check is slow and error-prone, and the rest of the session went into writing a reusable four-step recipe plus a baseline TSV into a project directory's CLAUDE.md.

*Wasted effort:* ~40% of browser turns. The double-encoded eval output, tab hunting and blind API-shape probing each cost a round trip before any real data came back.

**S30.** A long warm-outreach session (a note-taking app, a chat aggregator app, an archive from a professional social network). Browser work was a small but sharp slice: the agent researched a person's profile on the professional social network via a subagent using cached public data, the human doubted the result, and only after being explicitly told 'check hher profile with cuttle' did the agent attach to cuttle and read the live logged-in profile - which took a seven-call snapshot-and-grep dance.

*Wasted effort:* ~50% of the browser turns. Two turns lost to using the wrong (logged-out) source first, then five turns of snapshot/scroll/grep to extract three lines of text.

**S32.** Crypto holdings audit across EVM/Solana/Bitcoin/Tron. Cuttle entered when the user said 'could you also use cuttle and open this' with a link to a DeFi portfolio-aggregator page; the agent opened the page in a new tab, failed to re-fetch the site's API from inside the page (Turnstile/CORS), and worked around it by dumping the SSE response bodies the page had already received. It got the data and found a $2,610 lending-protocol position that the API-only agents had missed.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of browser turns - one failed in-page fetch plus five recovery calls to enumerate requests and dump response bodies.

**S33.** Short, explicitly cuttle-driven board check on a freelance marketplace ('use cuttle pls to check the missions'). The persistent session had gone logged-out, so the agent ran the documented login recipe - including a hand-rolled password injection through --raw eval - then pulled the board and application statuses via the in-page API and diffed against the committed baseline. It succeeded in ~12 calls with no retries.

*Wasted effort:* ~35%: the entire login sub-sequence (5 calls) existed only because the persistent login had lapsed, and the password step needed a bespoke DOM hack.

**S34.** Very long career-ops day (two compactions). Cuttle was used for repeated board/application checks on a freelance marketplace through the in-page API. The sharpest failure: a re-check fired while the human was reading a large link-aggregator site in the same browser, so the credentialed fetch executed on the link-aggregator's origin and failed with a jq parse error and a stack trace from that third-party site. The agent diagnosed it, found there is no per-tab targeting, borrowed the human's view via tab-select 0 and handed it straight back.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns in the re-check: one failed eval, one --help probe, one tab-list, plus the select/restore wrapper, all to run one query that had worked 40 minutes earlier.

**S36.** User asked the agent to "use cuttle browser and open the consumer banking app stuff to get the new txs". Agent ran `cuttle status`, attached playwright-cli over CDP, opened a tab on the consumer banking app, found the session had dropped to the passcode screen, handed off to the human via the VNC viewer, then ran a bespoke python CDP scraper (CUTTLE_CDP=...) that pulled 6,723 transactions across every wallet pocket. Fully succeeded; the rest of the session was ledger/SQL work.

*Wasted effort:* ~30% of browser turns. Of ~9 browser-facing calls, three existed only to compensate for missing perception (innerText eval, url eval, auth fetch probe) and one full turn was lost blocking on a human 'logged in' confirmation.

**S37.** Career/GTM session; at the end the user asked to refresh a freelance marketplace's mission board. Agent ran `cuttle up`, attached playwright-cli, opened the marketplace platform, then used the logged-in browser purely as an authenticated HTTP client - refreshing an auth token and fetching the marketplace's API endpoints from inside `--raw eval` - to pull 47 missions with rate bands. Succeeded on the first real attempt.

*Wasted effort:* ~15% of browser turns. Nothing failed, but 2 of 7 calls were probe/escaping iterations that a file-based eval or a `cuttle fetch` verb would have collapsed into one.

**S40.** GTM advisory session about accelerators; the user asked "q1: can you apply for me using cuttle?" for an accelerator program's application. Agent loaded the cuttle skill, ran `cuttle up` (which restarted the container), read the playwright-cli SKILL.md, attached over CDP, opened the apply form in a new tab, and read the accessibility snapshot to enumerate every form field. It then drafted all answers and stopped before filling, per the user's own no-submit-without-review rule (blocked on a founder video the user had to record).

*Wasted effort:* ~20-40% of the 5 browser-facing calls: one doc-read hop and one snapshot-file Read were pure orientation, and the silent container restart discarded prior browser state. The task itself never reached the fill/submit stage, so nothing was learned about typing into the form.

**S12.** A subagent investigating why the arm64/macOS-persona cuttle build times out on two third-party sites. It drove the local cuttle container directly over CDP (playwright-core attach at 127.0.0.1:24022) using three throwaway TypeScript probe scripts it wrote into the repo, plus docker exec/inspect for fonts, GPU and profile dirs. It succeeded: it proved both sites render fully on the macOS persona and that the failures were wait-condition/budget misconfigurations in the caller's own code, not stealth. Along the way it hit several cuttle/driver contract frictions.

*Wasted effort:* ~20% of its ~46 shell turns. One entire multi-minute probe run (6 navigations) was voided by an undocumented proxy-auth requirement, and roughly a third of the run existed only to build ad-hoc page-perception tooling cuttle does not provide.

**S16.** NOTE ON PROVENANCE: this session did not itself drive a browser - it is a friction-audit subagent (cwd = the cuttle repo) that read three real cuttle-driving sessions via an internal session-history query tool: S06 (playwright-cli, run-code-heavy, 123 driver calls), S03 (0.11.0 image upgrade eval, container lifecycle), and S24 (agent-browser driver). I set used_cuttle=true because every finding below is first-hand cuttle-driving evidence quoted with source message_ids; the friction belongs to those three sessions, not to this analyst. It succeeded at its analysis task. Attribute counts to the cited session ids, not to this one.

*Wasted effort:* Roughly 40-50% of browser turns in the analyzed sessions. In S06 the identified sinks are ~40 min of polling loops, ~3.5 min on the CodeMirror focus saga (13 calls), ~90 min of un-raw'd envelope output, and 21/40 run-code calls needing a file-write detour; in S03 ~10 min of blind cold-start sleeps plus a 21-call `docker rm -f` ritual that cuttle's own lifecycle verbs exist to remove.

**S16.** NOTE ON PROVENANCE: like the previous entry, this is a friction-audit subagent (same parent, cwd = the cuttle repo), not itself a browser-driving session. It analyzed session S05 (2026-08-07, run from a project directory): 601 Bash calls of which 183 were playwright-cli against cuttle, driving a cloud provider console + that provider's OAuth consent, then a note-taking app's developer console + that app's OAuth, to move a productivity-suite MCP server to user scope with creds in an internal tool. The browser work eventually succeeded but the agent was interrupted by the user mid-flail and handed one step back to the human. All findings below are first-hand evidence from S05, quoted with its message_ids.

*Wasted effort:* ~45-50% of browser turns - the audit's own tally is 80-90 of 183 browser calls as friction, with ~87 minutes of wall clock against an irreducible core of maybe 40 calls. Dominated by tab-foreground confusion (33 calls), the bbox+coordinate click ritual (48 calls, ~27 waste) and the 20-call tag-input saga.

**S05.** Long internal-tooling wiring session where the browser was used to configure a cloud provider's OAuth clients, publish a consent screen, complete two OAuth consents with that provider, drive an internal dashboard, and create a public OAuth integration on a note-taking app. The cloud-provider half succeeded; the note-taking-app half (Phase 2) was abandoned mid-form and handed back to the user, then retried later after an unrelated internal-tool upgrade. 601 Bash calls total, 183 of them playwright-cli, and exactly zero screenshots.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns. The agent had no way to see the page, so it hand-rolled a JS vision-and-click layer (132 of 183 calls were bespoke `--raw eval` DOM scrapes, 27 computed getBoundingClientRect coordinates, 21 issued raw mousemove/mousedown/mouseup), and re-derived the same foreground-tab diagnosis three separate times.

**S06.** GTM session for an internal project: sign into a SaaS console via code-host OAuth, submit the internal project's repo, configure the SaaS console's structured Dockerfile builder, run builds, cut releases, and repin the build URL after each release of the internal project. Also submitted to a second directory site and drove non-browser release engineering. The browser work succeeded but the Dockerfile-editor portions were repeatedly near-corrupted. 276 Bash calls, 117 playwright-cli, 2 screenshots.

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of browser turns. The two CodeMirror write episodes alone consumed ~20 calls and produced invalid JSON twice; roughly 10 more went to a mis-diagnosed 'stale cache' that was actually a server-side redirect.

**S01.** An 8-hour session driving a government tax-filing portal, a government social-security portal and a webmail client through cuttle to file a late annual income-tax declaration, then three periodic VAT declarations, three self-employed receipts, and a social-security quarterly declaration. It succeeded on every filing, but 892 tool calls were spent, 756 of them (85%) on browser interaction: 261 snapshot, 261 click, 234 eval, 91 find, 60 fill. A second cuttle instance was later spun up for a family member's tax account. The human took over by hand three separate times (typing a national tax id, dragging a slider, entering credentials) after the agent failed at each.

*Wasted effort:* ~45% of browser turns. The dominant cost is not any single failure but a structural one: reading page state cost more calls than changing it, and every state change needed a file round-trip to be believed.

**S02.** A career/positioning session (1034 calls) with two substantial cuttle bursts: creating and filling a company page on a professional social network for a personal consulting brand (including logo upload), and an iterative visual review of a locally-running Astro site (a third-party site) where the agent screenshotted the page repeatedly to answer the user's design feedback. Both browser goals were reached, but the social-network work required a mid-task driver switch forced by the user, a full form-refill after a save race, and a two-driver dance to get a single file uploaded; the site review required restructuring the user's dev server so the container could reach it at all.

*Wasted effort:* ~35-40% of browser turns. Most of it went to element-actionability workarounds on React, a silent upload failure, and container-to-host networking.

**S03.** An overnight cuttle 0.1.0 -> 0.11.x upgrade evaluation that turned into three cuttle releases. The agent drove cuttle-run Chromes over CDP (via hand-written Playwright probe scripts and a stealth harness) to compare fingerprints across images, personas and architectures, then found/fixed a daemon-killing panic and shipped 0.11.3 to prod. It succeeded, but only after two of its own releases (0.11.1, 0.11.2) shipped publicly carrying that crash, and after three wrong conclusions the agent itself had to retract (arm64 digest pin, WebGPU 'fix', 'panic confirmed fixed').

*Wasted effort:* ~35% of the browser/cuttle-diagnostic turns. The dominant sinks were: no way to ask cuttle whether the daemon survived a run (produced a false green that cost a whole release cycle), no stealth self-verify verb (six hand-written probe scripts, one of which returned a flatly wrong answer), and no test seam for the capture path (four failed panic repros).

**S04.** The agent logged into the user's real account on a freelance marketplace through cuttle (password-manager password + TOTP), audited a four-year-stale profile, and rebuilt it end to end: headline, bio, experience level, corrected work history, 27 skills, 8 project cards with generated logo images, links and resume. It then read the whole 120-job board through the site's private API and produced a bid recommendation plus a tracked TSV baseline. It succeeded, but through ~250 bash calls, 194 playwright-cli invocations, 76 hardcoded sleeps, and one misfired upload at a live account that the human took over by hand.

*Wasted effort:* ~40% of browser turns. Concentrated in: 76 sleep calls (~264s of pure guessing) because navigation-inside-eval has no wait verb, ~15 screenshot retries against a font-load timeout, ~10 calls discovering driver verb names, ~20 rebuilding stale modal/snapshot state, and 12+ scratchpad .js files written purely to escape shell quoting of eval payloads.
